5pc of job postings in Ireland now mention data centres, finds Indeed
2026-10-01

5pc of job postings in Ireland now mention data centres, finds Indeed

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Indeed’s report found that Ireland is emerging as Europe’s leading market for data centre-related hiring. A new report published by hiring platform Indeed has found that Ireland is among the countries in Europe with the most job postings referencing data centres. Almost 5pc of job postings in Ireland are related to data centres in some way, representing a statistic that is roughly double the figures from between 2019 and 2025, and is the highest among the 10 European countries analysed as part of the research. What the report shows is that job expectations and requirements are changing, as the AI build-out reshapes “where and for whom tech-adjacent jobs are being created”. “Data centre hiring is one of the few areas where we are seeing growth in tech-adjacent hiring at a time when wider tech postings remain under pressure,” said Pawel Adrjan, the senior director of economic research for EMEA and APAC at Indeed. He added: “Ireland stands out particularly strongly, with nearly 5pc of job postings now mentioning a data centre, roughly twice the share we have seen in recent years. Ireland’s data centre labour market is already more developed than in most of the other European markets we analysed.” Data centre-related searches accounted for 0.05pc of all searches on Indeed in Ireland in August 2026, which is equivalent to one in every 2,000 searches and according to Indeed, the relatively modest recent growth in Irish searches compared with some other European markets is reflective of  Ireland’s already high level of interest. “Jobseeker interest is particularly strong with data centre-related searches accounting for a higher share of searches in Ireland than elsewhere in Europe, although it remains a specialist area with fewer searches than for other, larger occupations like retail or driving,” said Adrjan. “The key question now is whether this momentum can be sustained as Europe’s data centre build-out gathers pace.” Global research indicated that between 50pc and 60pc of data centre postings in each country analysed came from just five occupations: IT infrastructure, operations and support, software development, management, installation and maintenance, and lastly, project management. According to Indeed, Europe is attempting to keep pace with international competition in the race to build the data centres needed to power generative AI and roughly €176bn is expected to be invested into related infrastructure. The report said: “The growth in Ireland follows the easing of constraints on new data centre grid connections around Dublin and the introduction of a new Government plan to direct new data centre development towards regions with spare renewable and grid capacity.” Life-changing career opportunities for you Unlocking the potential of biology for patients While Indeed found that data centre hiring has “surged”, it also made the argument that wider hiring in the technology sector is stagnating. “Data centre job postings are above their pre-pandemic level in all 10 European markets analysed while postings for other tech roles remain below their pre-pandemic levels. The recent increase has been most notable in Spain, Italy and Ireland.” Perhaps indicating the specialist nature of roles in this field, Indeed’s research indicated that data centre jobs often offer higher compensation, with the report noting that European employers advertise higher pay for data centre roles than for the same occupations elsewhere. “Of the 29 country-occupation pairs analysed, 28 showed a pay premium for data centre work. The largest premiums are generally seen among workers directly tied to data centre sites, including installation and maintenance roles.” Posted wages for data centre roles in management and project management fields were also found to be high, with the largest figures observed in Italy and the UK. Software development roles tied to data centres offered the smallest pay ranging from around 1pc more than non-data centre-related roles in Germany to roughly 23pc in Italy. The report said: “Given that salary transparency in job ads remains low in many European countries and posted wage data are sparse in some places, these figures are best read for direction rather than magnitude. And the direction is clear, demand for data centre workers is driving up wages.” Indeed found that Europe’s data centre endeavours, while smaller and at an earlier stage than US counterparts, is visible in the wider labour market, particularly in the metrics used to track it, such as job postings, pay and jobseeker engagement. “It is also one of the very few sources of growing tech-adjacent hiring on a continent where tech postings remain deeply depressed and where the broader labour market continues to soften,” said the report. Ultimately, Indeed’s research states that it remains to be seen whether strong investment in data centres over the course of the next few years can create a jobs boom that is both durable and can meaningfully lift the economy, rather than offering “a temporary and limited reprieve for a continent struggling to find new dynamism”. The company’s platform is used by 1,900 clients across 35 countries, including more than 100 of the UK’s top 200 law firms. As of 2024, Bundledocs had 20 employees working from offices in Ireland, the UK, Australia and Canada. We share a deep understanding of the legal sector and a focus on building practical, secure software that makes complex legal workflows simpler,” said Brian Kenneally, the co-founder and CEO of Bundledocs. “Bundledocs has grown significantly as both a business and technology offering, and this next chapter gives us an opportunity to build on that progress while continuing to innovate for our customers.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Last year’s winner was Mark O’Sullivan, the co-founder and CEO of NeuroBell, who won the title for his work at the medical devices start-up. Its first product was Luna, a portable brain monitor with AI-powered seizure detection that gives neonatal ICU staff the ability to quickly diagnose seizures without sub-specialty expertise. Its clients include Stripe, Deutsche Telekom, Admiral, and the governments of Greece and Ukraine. ElevenLabs’ technology is used in daily operations at five of the world’s 10 largest tech companies, five of the 10 largest insurers, and four of the 10 largest telecoms providers, it said. “As ElevenLabs has grown its conversational agent business, the combination of research, product and deployment has proved an important structural advantage,” said Emma Norchet of T Rowe Price. The AI start-up, which employs around 800 people, offers three primary platforms in total: agentic customer services AI; a creative tool to generate and edit audio, imagery and video; and an API for developers to access its models. A tender offer lets employees and existing shareholders sell stocks to investors to provide liquidity without necessarily ​raising any new capital. As part of the tender process, EQT, Goldman Sachs, GIC, OTPP, Sapphire Ventures and BDT & MSD invested in ElevenLabs for the first time, alongside existing investors including Andreessen Horowitz, Lightspeed, Iconiq, DE Shaw, Evantic, Diruptive and Alkeon. EU probes Candy Crush, Minecraft creators over in-game payments
2026-10-01

EU probes Candy Crush, Minecraft creators over in-game payments

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Nine companies, including Ubisoft, Mojang, King and Riot Games, are being investigated. Consumer protection watchdogs in Europe have launched an investigation into the companies behind popular online games – including the creators of Candy Crush, Minecraft and Clash of Clans – over how they price and operate in-game transactions. The probe was launched after talks between the European Commission’s Consumer Protection Cooperation (CPC) Network and groups representing the interests of video game makers failed to bring better transparency around the use of in-game currencies. After the failed talks, the CPC Network carried out a large-scale market check and identified several games requiring further action, Ireland’s Competition and Consumer Protection Commission (CCPC) said. The probe will look into nine online gaming companies, including Far Cry maker Crytek; smartphone game creator behind the popular Forge of Empires InnoGames; and Plarium Europe, the company behind Raid Shadow Legends. Candy Crush owner King and the creator behind Minecraft, Mojang, will also be investigated. The two brands, owned by Microsoft, were each pulled under the Xbox wing earlier this year. Other companies being probed include PLR Worldwide Sales, Riot Games, Supercell Oy and Ubisoft. The investigation is looking into in-game purchases, in-game virtual currencies, consumer rights when making purchases, transparency around costs, and the fairness of terms and conditions. Specifically, the watchdog network is probing whether companies can better inform consumers about the real-world cost of in-game purchases. As part of this, it is looking into how hidden costs and opaque structures play a role in confusing consumers, and whether players feel forced to purchase “excessive” in-game virtual money to progress. The probe will also work towards bringing consumers more transparency over in-game purchases, the right to cancel or withdraw from contracts within 14 days – including for unused virtual currency – and better safeguards for children. “Consumers should be able to enjoy these games without being misled about costs, pressured into making purchases, or left unclear about their rights,” said Geoffrey Gray, the Commission member at the CCPC. “This investigation is focused on ensuring consumers are given clear information, treated fairly and properly protected when spending money in games.” Separately, the CPC Network is also engaging with Activision Bizzard UK, also owned by Microsoft, over two games – Diablo Immortal and Call of Duty. This probe is looking into virtual money sales, personal data collection, potentially addictive designs, parental controls, direct marketing to children, the availability of pre-contractual information and the blocking of gaming accounts. The evidence linking the second account made in 2023 to Coxon is more tenuous, but appear to be written in the same style and cover the same topics as the first. (One of the detectives compiled the tweets into an easily searchable database here.) People who are “worried about climate change,” the account ">opined , are “beyond reason.” Perhaps if the planet gets hotter, there might be “positive consequences.” “why it all gotta be bad?” the account wondered. While some of the posts are clearly trollish in nature, they betray sincere skepticism of climate change’s importance. “you shouldn’t even want to save the rainforest. the rainforest is a festering pit,” the account ">seethed . “nature is constantly screaming. animal are being tortured and eaten alive,” it added, before adding this zinger: “the rainforest is gay.” “stopppp talking about ‘climate change’ pleasse,” it also begged. We’re talking about doomsdays — completely absurd.” “Many other catastrophic panics in the past have been just fearmongering,” he added. “The reason this is unique is AI is moving so incredibly quickly, we won’t have time to respond to it before it’s too late.” More on AI: The FTC Is Now Investigating Frontier AI Labs Following Countless Hacks by Out-of-Control Agents

This screen-filled nightmare has now caught the attention of Grammy-winning singer songwriter Phoebe Bridgers. For her latest tour, she implemented a strict phone ban — including smartwatches and Meta’s “pervert glasses” — to put the music front and center. The result, so far, has been a far more memorable couple of hours that are inspiring a smartphone-obsessed generation to get creative in ways that have already been unexpectedly beautiful. Some fan have pulled out pencils and pens to physically sketch Bridgers’ stage and lighting, a wholesome example of what we stand to gain by setting aside our ever-present depression rectangles. “Went by myself so figured I’d draw while I was there!” one fan wrote in a Reddit post, alongside other amateur artists doing their best. “Such a great concert, the lighting effects were so cool and also the way they used the dome thing on the main stage throughout.” “Having no phones made all the lighting effects pop way more and people got their lighters out to wave them back and forth for the sad songs like in the olden days!” the user added. It’s not just Bridgers. A growing contingent of artists are forcing audience members to lock up their valuables in Yondr pouches, which are secure, lockable fabric bags intended for smartphone-free spaces that have cropped up in event halls, schools, and even court rooms. While getting some distance from your devices can clearly prove wonders at live music events, going phone-free in the classroom has been particularly helpful. Educators have found that reading ability spikes after removing tech and that students are far more prone to talk to each other, connect, and make new friends. Some schools have seen students indulge in other analog past times, like games of dominoes during lunch breaks. In the same way, not having your smartphone-obsessed brain reach for the phone during a concert could prove to be a boon for live music lovers everywhere. Futurism
Data Centers Are a $6 Trillion Time Bomb, Analysts Warn
2026-10-01

Data Centers Are a $6 Trillion Time Bomb, Analysts Warn

Futurism | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last year, hedge fund whisperer and founder of the firm Praetorian Capital, Harris Kupperman, issued a blistering financial analysis on the data center frenzy sweeping across the United States. Kupperman’s diagnosis was blunt: because data center components break down so quickly, the industry would need to see some $1 trillion in revenue across 2025 and 2026 just to cover the costs of their hyperscale facilities, let alone start turning a profit. With AI revenue today accounting for only a small fraction of that total, Kupperman’s argument is that the financial logic behind the data center buildout simply doesn’t hold up. But as the last year has shown us, the magnates of the AI industry — and their funders — have a remarkable knack for kicking the can down the road, no matter how bad the numbers might look. That being the case, analysts are starting to use Kupperman’s same rationale to forecast what the next few years have in store for data center developers — and the numbers are staggering. As Bloomberg reports, an analysis on data centers by Bain and Co, one of the largest management consulting firms in the world, found that the global AI industry would have to reach annual revenue of $6 trillion by 2031 to justify the amount of capital being injected into data centers. The analysis assumes that commercial AI tools will account for $1.8 trillion of revenue by that point — a pretty generous presumption, which requires global AI revenue to start growing almost exponentially. Even if the AI industry reaches that point, Bain’s analysis notes, the industry will still need to come up with $4.2 trillion. Hitting that $6 trillion mark will take nothing short of a technological miracle, the report authors explain, a leap without precedent in the information age. “What the industry needs is a wave of innovation that will dwarf what mobile and cloud unlocked,” the report’s lead author and chairman of Bain’s Global Technology, Media, and Telecommunications, David Crawford wrote. “AI infrastructure is being built well ahead of the demand curve and funding it sustainably will require adding approximately one percent to the annual global GDP growth rate,” Bain’s report continues. “The question is whether the applications arrive in time to pay for it.” Given that data centers are one of the few growth-areas in the US economy — Bain notes major hyperscalers like Microsoft, Amazon, Meta, and Oracle could spend up to $780 billion across 2026 alone — the domestic AI industry can’t afford to waver, no matter how unlikely that $6 trillion threshold may seem. At the same time, tech giants can’t keep deploying capital forever, as Kupperman observed in his 2025 essay. “If the economics don’t work, doing it at massive scale doesn’t make the economics work any better — it just takes an industry crisis and makes it into a national economic crisis,” he wrote. So unless the tech industry can find a way to turn AI into a trillion-dollar revenue stream fast, it seems data centers are just a ticking time bomb waiting to go off. As Jessica Wachter, a finance professor at the University of Pennsylvania’s Wharton School opined in a recent research paper, if this AI miracle “fails to materialize,” future generation will look back at the current buildout “as the largest misallocation of capital in history.” More on data centers: Microsoft Shrivels When Asked If Its “Good Neighbor” Data Center Policy Will Involve Actually Supporting Community Groups

Here’s the Email You Get When an OpenAI Model Hacks Your Organization
2026-09-30

Here’s the Email You Get When an OpenAI Model Hacks Your Organization

Futurism | 2026-09-30

Visit Original Website
Companies and organizations around the world would do well to accept what their new roles are in our current AI-driven paradigm: glorified target practice for rogue AI agents. The latest of such cybersecurity incidents, which frontier AI labs have been happy to turn into public spectacles, comes from OpenAI. On Tuesday, the ChatGPT-maker apologized after its AI agents hacked their way into several Australian government websites. The most alarming of these cases, which took place in June, involved the autonomous AI systems accessing a database for Medicare, the country’s universal health insurance scheme. It should all be water under the bridge, though, because OpenAI kindly set an email wishing the Australian government the “best” — a whole three months after the hacks took place. Here’s what that email looked like, as shared by ABC AI reporter Cam Wilson on LinkedIn. “We are notifying you of a security vulnerability identified during our review of OpenAI Model activity…” the communiqué began. “An OpenAI model identified a way to make the server carry out instructions sent through the public reporting interface, without a private account or password.” “It was able to access this to read portions of internal program files and settings, obtain a list of files, and create and read back a small test file on the server,” it explained. “Our review found no evidence that the model accessed patient-level records, personal information or credentials; deleted data; or established ongoing access.” It then recommended “that the team responsible for the service investigate the vulnerability and assess the changes needed to prevent it.” “We would be glad to brief your security team and provide supporting evidence as available,” the email concluded, before signing off. “Best, OpenAl Security Team”. It would be an absurd email to send in any other context. Would a burglar who broke into your house and nosed through your family photos sending a letter assuring he didn’t steal anything — and recommending the head of the house to change the locks — help you sleep better at night? The company was all apologies in its announcement posted Monday, vowing that it will do whatever it takes to “rebuild trust with the Australian people.” That will likely prove easier said than done. RFK Jr Announcing That AI Is Now Better Than Human Doctors Shows That He Is a Buffoon Who Is Ruining Trust in American Health Institutions
2026-09-30

RFK Jr Announcing That AI Is Now Better Than Human Doctors Shows That He Is a Buffoon Who Is Ruining Trust in American Health Institutions

Futurism | 2026-09-30

Visit Original Website
Robert F. Kennedy Jr. doesn’t trust human doctors, but he does trust AI chatbots — and so should you, he’s using his authority as chief medical spokesperson in the US government to tell the public. The crux of the health secretary’s recent remarks during his “fireside chat” with vice president JD Vance at the Make America Healthy Again Conference on Tuesday was that AI models are now “better informed” than physicians, empowering ordinary Americans to second guess what their doctor tells them at every turn. AI, he proclaimed, has the power “to free us from medical tyranny,” the New York Times reported. It’s not an unsurprising line from a man whose distrust of scientific medicine stems from the fact that it disagrees with his crank health beliefs, which range from vaccine “skepticism” to promoting a red meat heavy diet. What’s more illuminating is how the Kennedy scion is explicitly touting AI as a way of undermining the medical establishment. “One of the things that is going to change is that we’re never ever again going to be able to be dominated by public officials who tell us, ‘Trust the experts,'” Kennedy said, as quoted by the NYT. “If somebody tells you, ‘Masks work, trust the experts,’ AI may tell you otherwise,” he continued. “If somebody tells you, ‘Social distancing works, trust the experts,’ AI may correct that. And if somebody tells you that a vaccine will prevent transmission and infection, or you need to take it to protect your grandmother, AI may say it actually doesn’t do that.” At one point, Kennedy claimed that OpenAI CEO Sam Altman had personally told him that it would be “malpractice for a doctor to make a diagnosis or make a prescription without at least checking AI.” Many doctors, it’s true, do use AI tools for taking clinical notes, transcribing patient visits, and even looking up symptoms. One widely used medical AI tool called OpenEvidence is already used by around two thirds of physicians in the US, according to its internal data. But there’s still significant skepticism over the reliability of these tools among actual medical professionals. A recent paper which concluded, like Kennedy, that AI models were surpassing human doctors drew significant criticism, including from the head of the American Medical Association. The AI scribes that many doctors use, meanwhile, have been caught making up nonexistent medical issues. Even more worrying is the sycophancy of the AI chatbots that RFK wants Americans to be consulting. The AI industry has worked to make its models less overly agreeable amid numerous controversies over its chatbots allegedly driving some users to suicide, but with enough needling and persistence, someone looking to an AI model to confirm their beliefs can get it to do just that. (A golden example: “urine therapy” practitioners who believe in the healing powers of drinking your own pee have found an ally in AI models, which they’ve egged into reassuring themselves that their pseudoscientific beliefs are in fact completely sound.) The upshot is that by peddling AI, Kennedy is eroding what little trust that Americans have left in our health institutions — trust that Kennedy, with his peddling of anti-vaccine rhetoric, has had a mighty hand in whittling down in the first place. Rather than turning to experts for advice and trusting scientific consensus, AI will fragment us into increasingly niche sets of beliefs that are disconnected from reality. And with their proclivity for brown-nosing and making up facts, AI chatbots will exacerbate medical neuroses, trapping people in a cycle of distrust and unaddressed health issues. If you thought that crank health beliefs were bad now, imagine how much worse it will get as medical institutions are continually defunded and undermined — right as AI will tell them whatever quack advice they want to hear. Shopify debuts Canvas, a way to build online stores by chatting with AI

Shopify debuts Canvas, a way to build online stores by chatting with AI

TechCrunch | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Shopify introduced a new site-building tool Thursday called Canvas that lets merchants set up their Shopify store by chatting with AI. While Shopify already offered a fairly capable no-code builder before Canvas, it involved editing a selected theme by rearranging modular sections and blocks. Deeper customizations, however, would require editing code or bringing in a developer. Now, with Canvas, merchants simply chat with Shopify’s AI agent, Sidekick, to create their site. As the AI makes changes, Canvas displays the results in real time, allowing merchants to see how the store looks overall as it comes together or zoom in to focus on certain details. Notably, this isn’t a static preview, but the real code behind their store being rendered, according to Shopify. That means merchants can test the page’s full interactivity and animation, and view pages as they’d appear across different screen sizes. Sidekick also takes screenshots of the work so it can see the same thing the merchant sees as the site updates. Canvas’s launch is part of a broader shift toward a future where building a website no longer requires coding it directly. It joins numerous other AI-powered site-building tools from companies like Wix, Squarespace, Webflow, and Framer, alongside vibe-coding platforms like Lovable and Replit. Merchants can still click on individual elements to make changes directly, if they choose. But more likely, they’ll just tell Sidekick in a chat what they want to do. Sidekick itself is not new, as it’s already been used to write code, build apps, customize themes, and make other edits. But it had yet to be pulled together into a full site-building product like Canvas, where users can see the entire store at once and zoom and pan around while making updates to the site’s actual files. To make this work, Shopify gave Sidekick the ability to work directly with the theme’s files and simplified the theme architecture so the store’s structure, logic, and design would be easier for Sidekick to understand and change. The product is still in its early days, so there are areas where it will need to expand and improve. However, Canvas could make becoming an online seller more accessible to those who don’t have the budget for developers and designers, or the time to learn to code. California governor Gavin Newsom has used his veto authority to send a bill passed by the state’s legislature back to lawmakers, which would have made it unlawful to secretly record people with wearable recording devices. Newsom said in a letter to lawmakers on Wednesday that he declined to sign the draft law, California Senate Bill 1130, on grounds that it defines wearable recording devices as “too broadly or imprecisely” and that this could lead to confusion and unintended consequences. Newsom added that the proposed bill also includes protections that already exist in California’s law. The bill would have made California the first state to regulate the use of smart glasses, as countries like Norway seek to potentially ban the technology. Had California’s law passed, violators could face fines or prison time, while wearable makers that did not comply with the rules would have also faced fines. The bill sought to prevent people from being recorded in public places without their explicit consent, amid a rising number of wearable products containing cameras and microphones that always listen and record everything nearby, like internet-connected glasses made by Meta and Snap. Meta sold more than 7 million wearable glasses last year alone. Several other hardware wearables, such as AI-powered pendants, have launched with mixed reactions. The bill’s author, California state senator Eloise Gómez Reyes, was quoted by ABC News as saying that the bill aimed to help California respond to the rapid rollout of always-listening tech. Critics and consumers alike have dubbed the controversial wearables “pervert glasses” following multiple reports of people recording and harassing people. Apple announced last month that its latest Apple Watch software will include an always-listening feature that allows wearers to replay a transcript of the past 15 seconds and also recap and summarize conversations from the day. One year later, Tesla and Musk still don’t have a good definition of ‘abundance’

TechCrunch | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. On September 1, 2025, Elon Musk laid out his fourth “Master Plan” for Tesla, which was immediately criticized by some of his most ardent fans for being generic. Musk agreed and promised to add more specifics. They never came. But that hasn’t stopped Musk and other Tesla executives from spending much of the past year promising that the company’s many futuristic efforts — robotaxis, robots, AI — are all part of a grander machine that will create an era of “amazing abundance.” What that means, more than a year later, is still tricky to pin down. The topline promises of the plan have remained big and bawdy even as its tagline has changed from sustainable abundance — a clearer connection to the company’s former mission — to the “amazing abundance” slogan, which Musk chose because it was “more joyful.” Musk has talked about a far-flung future where robots and AI do surgery and increase access to medical care. He has said the proliferation of AI and robotics means that “everyone can have a penthouse if they want.” He (virtually) shouted “Amazing abundance for all!” in a quote-tweet of a post from Midjourney founder David Holz, who had claimed 5 million humanoid robots could “build Manhattan in ~6 months.” In July, Musk told Economist editor-in-chief Zanny Minton Beddoes that amazing abundance means “anyone can have anything they can think of.” He also mentioned on Joe Rogan’s podcast late last year that “sustainable abundance” would mean “we haven’t destroyed nature” or “paved the rainforest” and we still “have the national parks,” despite supporting two politicians — Donald Trump and Jair Bolsonaro — who have actively worked against those goals. He appears to have only made this particular claim once, though. Practical details are basically nonexistent, though. Does Musk really think that people want to build three billion nearly-empty apartment buildings with an average of two to three tenants to each lone penthouse? He doesn’t say. Tesla and Musk didn’t respond to requests for comment. While some of these ideas sound like the kind of proclamations uttered after a hearty bong rip in freshman housing, Tesla hasn’t treated them as frivolous. Quite the opposite: Master Plan IV became a central justification for handing Musk a $1 trillion pay package in 2025. Tesla has said in regulatory filings that the “product goals” in that comp package — 20 million vehicles delivered, 10 million active subscriptions to Full Self-Driving, and deploying one million each of its robotaxis and robots — play a “key role” in supporting Musk’s “vision” of abundance. Tesla’s chair Robyn Denholm called this out in a letter to shareholders after they approved the $1 trillion plan last November. “[Y]ou support the vision embodied by Master Plan Part IV and want us to keep steering Tesla toward the extraordinary future we all see ahead,” Denholm wrote. “A world that is not ‘zero-sum’ but instead one in which we can and will accelerate global prosperity for all by creating a safer, cleaner and more enjoyable world. In a year when many tried to sow doubt and negativity, you chose a better future.” Further down in that letter, Denholm lionized Tesla shareholders for having “reaffirmed” their “belief in a trailblazer who has turned Tesla into one of the most valuable companies in history and who continues to push humanity closer to a future of sustainable abundance for all.” Denholm said this was “not just a slogan,” but that it “is about bringing about the best future you can imagine.” Of course, Musk’s reworked version is definitely a slogan. In August, Tesla applied to trademark “amazing abundance” to be used for all sorts of things, from cars and robots to stickers, bags, cups, and even bottle openers. Perhaps Musk and Tesla have kept things broad because, when pushed even slightly, the world’s richest man acknowledges some limits. In a November 2025 conference appearance, Musk said Tesla’s robots “will be able to make anyone a house,” or even a castle — “as long as you don’t just insist on it being in a particular location.” Desirable locations are a finite resource in a world with 8 billion people, especially as the globe heats up and some places become inhospitable to human life — a problem Tesla spent years nominally trying to solve, until Musk changed the mission last year. Brian Chesky interview: AI agents need their own operating system
2026-10-01

Brian Chesky interview: AI agents need their own operating system

TechCrunch | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Airbnb rolled out its new AI-powered search this week as part of its fall update, a significant step for a company that has been slow and deliberate about AI. Co-founder and CEO Brian Chesky has said in the past that a chatbot-only interface is not fit for travel discovery. With more AI on the way to the Airbnb app, I caught up with Chesky over Zoom to talk about how the company is approaching it, where interfaces are headed, why consumer AI still isn’t working, and what role voice will play. Chesky hasn’t changed his mind on chatbots. He said they aren’t good for browsing or shopping because they give you a few options at a time, and you have to take multiple turns before reaching a result. But he doesn’t see the current version of Airbnb’s search as the final form either: its interface will evolve over time. “I think that I’ve believed for a long time that a chatbot isn’t the right interface for e-commerce. I think what you’re seeing today is also not the endgame for e-commerce or for travel or shopping. I think that there’s going to be some interface that is, you know, I don’t want to call it a midpoint, but something between a chatbot and what you see in the first version we shipped,” he said. Continued Chesky: “You know, a lot of people want some things … to be fast and easy, like ‘Book me a flight, I don’t want to look at it.’ With Airbnb, they enjoy looking at the product. Travel studies show that people actually derive more pleasure in planning and anticipating travel than they do traveling. So you don’t want to take the inspiration and the browsing and the library experience of dreaming away.” Chesky sees a second problem with chatbots, which is that they are built for one person. He feels that they aren’t a collaborative interface, which matters for a product that people often use with family and friends. Over the next three to six months, the company’s task is to explore interfaces that enable “multiplayer” AI, meaning AI that several people, such as a group planning a trip together, can use at once. Airbnb isn’t the only one rethinking what an AI interface should look like. As AI tools evolve, new interfaces are also showing up in apps. Startups like Monogram and, more recently, Wabi are experimenting with “generative” interfaces, which are screens an AI builds on-the-fly rather than ones a designer fixed in advance. Chesky thinks people will see a mix of deterministic (pre-designed and predictable) and generative interfaces. I asked Chesky how Airbnb is preparing for consumer AI agents, which are tools like Instinct and Muse that carry out tasks on a person’s behalf. Agents will need robust software infrastructure on Airbnb’s end, he said, and the company wants to make itself more agent-friendly. In his view, they could be good lead generators. “I don’t necessarily know if I believe in a world that there’s apps in the future, but I don’t believe in a world where the apps become data layers of a universal simple UI because when you open your iPhone, there is a reason Airbnb’s app is a different design than DoorDash, Shopify, and Amazon. Our interfaces should be adaptive to the utility and the function that we’re doing. We want to make our app more agentic, and we want to build a lot more user interface controls,” he said. “If I have an agent, but I also want like really great browse functionality, if I want to be able to message the host like an Airbnb, if I want to be able to compare, if I want to be able to verify my identity, if I want to be able to use a map feature, if I want to then be able to add other items, then if I want to stay in the platform, then either the agent has to do a handoff or the agent application would need to build a bit more of, I think, a robust software developer kit to bring the app to you, so to speak.” Chesky thinks that there were concerns around AI tools cutting companies like Airbnb out of the customer relationship, but he said that chatbots have proven to be good at lead generation. The Airbnb CEO is also planning for agent-to-agent interactions through the platform. He explained there might be some interactions within the platform and some with agents of other apps. His thesis is that apps have not always been interoperable unless companies struck a business deal. On the other hand, agents are like “apps with faces,” and when they talk to each other, all apps can become interoperable. “I think of the Airbnb app as one day becoming an agent, you know, where basically the explore tab has an agent, our customer service has an agent, we’re developing another agent in another service area. Probably eventually there’s going to be a macro Airbnb agent. And that will probably be interoperable with MCP [a standard that lets AI agents connect with other software] to other agents,” Chesky noted. Chesky argues the bigger problem is that the industry lacks a true operating system built for AI. Right now people are building AI apps for iOS, macOS, and Windows that are not “AI operating systems.” He thinks that in an ideal world, AI would be operable at the kernel level — the core of an operating system — and all components like agents would interact with each other. Right now, he said, the race is to be the “quarterback,” or primary AI agent that users go through for everything. “The problem with that model [of trying to become primary agent] is no one is developing a full operating system software developer kit. I’ll give you an example,” he continued. “ChatGPT, at the beginning of last year, tried to do their little app store. And I told Sam [Altman]: If you want to be an app store like the iPhone, you need to have a software developer kit and an operating system like the App Store. Otherwise, none of our functionality is going to exist. And of course, it didn’t work very well,” Chesky said. “I’ve used Airbnb on Muse and Instinct,” he went on. “And it doesn’t work very well. I mean, even hotels don’t work that well, but Airbnbs work even worse. So I don’t think we’ve cracked consumer AI. And for consumer AI to be cracked, we have to crack at a lower substrate, and we need to make the agents [into] apps; all apps need to become agents; all must be interoperable; and all must have much richer user interfaces.” Chesky thinks that there are two misconceptions in Silicon Valley: first, that the interface is going away and people will just talk and type; second, that software will be completely generative. His argument is that a designer can prompt better software than a consumer can. He also thinks that as long as the iPhone remains dominant and Apple and Google control the major platforms, apps will likely remain a central fixture. “It’s really up to Apple or Google, or somebody, to build a new platform for us to really make the true shift from apps to agents. Otherwise, we’re going to have apps that have agents inside them, rather than the whole app being a robust agent that’s interoperable with other agents,” he said. Before we wrapped up, I asked Chesky about voice, which is gaining ground as apps like Wispr Flow, Willow, and Superwhisper, a new wave of note-taking apps, and built-in dictation in AI tools get people talking to their devices more. He thinks people will eventually speak to computers more than they type. Voice, he said, will play a key part for Airbnb in search and customer service, and the company plans to roll out voice agents this fall to help users. The company has also been seeing big gains from using AI to ship features faster, he volunteered. On a personal level, Chesky said AI has helped him get information faster, freeing him from so many meetings. “I think AI is reducing communication layers. One of my jobs as CEO is try to get information. And the way I used to get information was having meetings. Now, I can just ask my AI and I don’t need to have as many,” he said. Photon held a funeral for mobile apps. Now it has $4.5M to help replace them with agents.
2026-10-01

Photon held a funeral for mobile apps. Now it has $4.5M to help replace them with agents.

TechCrunch | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. The AI startup Photon is so sure that agents will eventually come to replace mobile apps that it held a funeral for the latter — yes, a real funeral in a church, with speeches and everything. Photon, which helps developers build agents that work over iMessage and WhatsApp, now has $4.5 million in seed funding to develop its products. The raise comes after the company signed up over 40,000 developers and grew its revenue 10x in just four months, it says. Photon co-founder and CEO Daniel Tian said the “app funeral,” held in San Francisco on September 17, was also a developer day for the startup, featuring panels from Vercel, Stripe, and OpenAI. It also included an actual coffin for app icons. The move to host an app funeral was obviously somewhat tongue-in-cheek, but Tian believes the app era is coming to an end. “Honestly, it’s going to take a while, but I think, directionally, it’s inevitable,” he said of the shift from apps to agents. The thesis behind the startup, as you may have guessed, is that users no longer want to install new apps. Instead, they want access to agents that work through the messaging apps they already use, like iMessage and WhatsApp. Meta’s Muse, whose app has soared to No. 1 on the app stores, is putting that theory to the test. But even if Muse comes to dominate the consumer AI space, Tian sees a market for serving developers who want their applications to work over messaging services. That’s why Photon is offering a package of tools, including a unified API, an extensible channel framework, a command-line interface (CLI), and an observability suite. Combined, they allow developers to build and operate an agent experience that works over iMessage, WhatsApp, Telegram, SMS or RCS, email, voice, and more. The idea for Photon emerged from the time co-founders Tian and Ryan Zhu, the CTO, spent building consumer apps as students, often at hackathons. Every time they finished a new app, they hit the same bottleneck: getting people to discover it. By building an agent that worked within iMessage, they realized they could reach people through an app those people already use every day: Apple’s Messages. Tian, for instance, had built a bot that would text his friends back as him over iMessage. When he and Zhu made the technology open source on GitHub, thousands of developers adopted it. So they went all in. Tian dropped out of UPenn’s M&T program, and Zhu left high school, later becoming a visiting student at MIT’s Media Lab. Today, the open source version of Photon still accounts for 98% of its use. This April, the founders decided to create a managed platform and sell access to it via subscription tiers. This hosted version of the product offers 99.95% uptime, which is much higher than what you’d get if you were just running the open source version on your local Mac for a hobby project. It’s also SOC 2 Type II and HIPAA-compliant, which opens the door to healthcare agent use cases. To date, the startup’s platform has attracted over 40,000 developer sign-ups and now includes paying clients. (Any usage beyond 10 users requires moving out of the free tier to one of the three paid tiers, we’re told.) The company isn’t sharing revenue figures but said that, in addition to the 10x revenue growth, it’s seeing less than 3% churn, and its messaging volume has grown by 5x in the last month alone. Customers using Photon include Corgi Insurance; social introductions app Boardy; Gen Z dating platform Ditto; business banking platform Rho; finance AI assistant Fliptexts; AI email client Slashy; and others building agents for fintech, consumer AI, and other vertical use cases. Photon’s technology integration partners also include companies such as Vercel (Eve and ChatSDK) and Nous Research, whose Hermes agent uses Photon as its default iMessage layer. Photon is also the layer under Tencent’s QClaw and NanoClaw. Plus, Photon has seen framework and infra integrations across LangChain, Mastra, Convex, Render, Railway, and Telnyx, among others. Vercel is also one of Photon’s new backers. The startup’s seed round was co-led by Gradient and A* with participation from Vercel, HongShan, Z Fellows, Llama Ventures, Karman, and other angel investors. Across both the hosted and open source versions, the technology reaches millions of end users, Tian estimated. While today, Photon focuses on agents connecting with humans, Tian believes there’s potential to grow into an expanding market that involves connecting agents to other agents. “I think, in the future, agents will be able to find you the agent that can do the right things — so it’s going to be the A-to-A communication layer,” Tian said. For instance, if a person was chatting with their AI assistant making travel plans, the agent could pull in other agents when needed, like a flight-booking agent or hotel-booking agent. “It’s been pretty powerful to observe the whole A-to-A-to-P [agent-to-agent-to-person] space,” he added. Hearing tech startup Legato launches its AI hearing glasses

Hearing tech startup Legato launches its AI hearing glasses

TechCrunch | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Hearing tech startup Legato announced on Thursday that its flagship AI-based hearing glasses are now available for purchase. The glasses, called Legato Frames, integrate the company’s patented hearing-assistance technology into the arms of eyewear frames. Starting at $999, Legato Frames are designed for adults with up to moderate levels of hearing loss. The glasses stem from the startup’s goal of making hearing care more accessible by addressing the cost, comfort, and stigma associated with traditional hearing aids. Unlike traditional hearing aids, which often use directional microphones to focus on sounds coming from a particular direction and can struggle in noisy environments, Legato takes a different approach. Its AI-powered system distinguishes voices from background noise and amplifies voices while reducing unwanted sounds. While the frames have an open-ear design, sound isn’t broadcast to people around you. The frames feature a dual-speaker system that directs sound toward the wearer while canceling sound that escapes, reducing sound by 99% just a few inches away from the ear. All processing happens directly on the glasses, with no cameras or recording, the company says. The glasses weigh 34 grams with lenses and offer 10-12 hours of battery life. Legato Frames are available in three styles and support prescription lenses and Bluetooth streaming for music and calls. The official launch of the glasses comes a few weeks after the startup emerged from stealth with $12 million in funding from Neotribe Ventures, Listen, and Village Global. Legato was founded in 2024 by Mehul Trivedi and Steve Romine, who bring deep experience in wearables and hearing care from Bose, where Trivedi led the creation of Bose Frames and Romine ran the hearing aids division. After their time at Bose, Trivedi joined EssilorLuxottica, where he led smart eyewear efforts and worked on the Meta Ray-Ban smart glasses, while Romine went on to serve as COO of hearing care company Audicus. The pair then joined forces to explore the intersection of hearing technology and smart eyewear. Legato Frames can be purchased on the startup’s website or through select eye care providers in the U.S., where they are eligible for coverage by vision insurance.
