The True Story Behind Netflix's The Widower and the Many Wives of Thomas Randolph
2026-10-01

The True Story Behind Netflix's The Widower and the Many Wives of Thomas Randolph

TIME | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Kring-Schreifels is a contributor for TIME. Francis and Thomas Randolph in The Widower Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Kring-Schreifels is a contributor for TIME. On the night of May 8, 2008, Thomas Randolph called Las Vegas police with a disconcerting alert. “My wife’s been shot,” he told the 9-11 dispatcher. “There’s a guy in my house and I shot him.” That night, Randolph and his sixth wife, Sharon Causse, had gone out for a Mother’s Day dinner. But once they returned home, Randolph stayed in the car to listen to the radio, letting Sharon go inside by herself. Minutes later, an intruder wearing a ski mask and carrying a bag of jewelry fired a couple of fatal shots at Causse. Upon hearing the gun discharge, Randolph later said, he entered the house, relaying to police in a walkthrough that he’d found Sharon lying on the floor bleeding out. At that moment, after seeing a moving shadow, he grabbed a gun from a nearby room and reactively shot the intruder multiple times, killing him with a bullet to the head, before clearing the rest of the house and calling authorities. The initial layout of the case was shocking, but it also seemed cut and dry. At least until detective Dean O’Kelley began examining the chain of events a little more closely. Why was the intruder’s ski mask on the garage floor without any blood on it? How were multiple rounds shot in the hallway but no bullet casings were left there? Why did a neighbor hear gunshots at 8:33 p.m., but Randolph’s 9-11 call didn’t occur until 8:45 p.m.? The next day, veteran Dateline NBC producer Dan Slepian heard about the murders from O’Kelley’s colleague, who offered an intriguing message: “You might want to pay attention to this one.” Slepian had previously worked alongside the LVPD homicide unit, filming real-time investigations, so he was accustomed to documenting cases as they unfolded. This seemed like another prime opportunity to do just that. Except when Slepian and O’Kelley teamed up, neither realized they were embarking on a 15-year journey—one that would eventually expose the truth about Randolph, a former paralegal who received life-insurance payouts after three of his previous wives all died under suspicious circumstances. Over the years, Randolph, known as the Black Widower, would escape two death penalty convictions, repeatedly finding ways around the legal system for as long as he could. That lengthy investigation, along with Randolph’s sinister history, is the subject of a new Netflix documentary, The Widower: ‘Til Death Do Us Part. Directed by Chris Smith, the executive producer of Tiger King, the documentary leans on Slepian to tell the complete story, featuring his real-time footage of interviews with Randolph, witnesses across the country, and family members of his victims, as authorities worked to eventually convict him of murder, put him behind bars, and keep him there. “Normally, the more you investigate, the closer you get to an answer,” Slepian tells TIME. “This investigation did the exact opposite. Every answer widened the frame. Another marriage, another state, another death, another witness. And that's when I realized that what we were covering wasn’t just a single murder case, but a pattern that had been unfolding over decades.” As Slepian and O’Kelley began digging more into the Mother’s Day murders, the pair found more alarming circumstances and reasons to believe Randolph had been lying about the sequence of events. Causse's daughter Colleen told them that Randolph had become the beneficiary of her mom’s will three months into their marriage. He’d also taken out a $350,000 life insurance policy on her—raising Colleen’s suspicions even further. The pair also traveled to North Carolina to interview family members of the intruder, Mike Miller, with whom Randolph had developed a working relationship. According to his brothers, Miller had become Randolph’s handyman and was constantly on the phone with him about various things—inlcuding one call in which, according to Miller’s friend, Randolph asked Miller if he’d be interested in killing his wife. It was juicy hearsay, and key context, but the prosecutor’s office declined to issue an arrest warrant. They’d need to do more digging. The rest of the documentary unfolds much like O’Kelley and Slepian’s real-time investigation, criss-crossing the country and unearthing new information about Randolph’s past that eerily informs the Causse case. “That kind of documentation as things unfold adds a value to not only the journalism but to the storytelling itself,” Slepian says. “There's a layer of suspense because the viewer understands that neither we nor the detectives have any idea what's about to happen.” The reality was more vast than either Slepian or O’Kelley could believe. It started with a visit to Randolph’s first wife, Kathy, who shared that after marrying Randolph in 1975, he’d taken out a life insurance policy on her. After having two kids with him, Kathy filed for divorce and remarried to Steve Thomas. Unbeknownst to her at the time, Thomas told O’Kelley and Slepian that Randolph had previously tried to groom to kill Kathy, but he refused to cooperate. Then a larger trend emerged. In 1983, on the same day his divorce to Kathy was finalized, Randolph married his second wife, Becky Gault. Three years later, Slepian and O’Kelly would find out  that Gault had died of a gunshot to the head inside her Utah home. Her death was initially ruled a suicide. But in 1988, her death was reclassified and Randolph was put on trial for murder, after he’d collected a $500,000 life-insurance payout. It didn’t help his case that his friend and handyman, Eric Tarantino, testified that Randolph had hired him to kill Gault and had been planning her murder for six months before Tarantino eventually backed out of the plan. A year later, Randolph was acquitted after his lawyers convinced the jury that Gault had died by suicide, though Randolph was convicted of witness tampering (which would later be expunged) after he was found guilty of offering an undercover cop money to kill Tarantino. Thomas Randolph and Becky Gault Courtesy of Netflix Three more marriages ensued, each culminating under strange, sometimes fatal circumstances. There was Gaynal Allmon, who was “stupid enough to marry him,” she says in the doc, after admitting he’d also taken out a life insurance policy on her. They divorced before anything nefarious happened, but Francis Randolph wasn’t as fortunate. Throughout their marriage, Frances had a heart condition that required surgery, so Randolph himself selected the doctor to perform the operation. She made it out to the recovery floor, but then Randolph called her family and indicated she’d taken a turn for the worse. “He insists she died from malpractice,” Slepian says in the doc. “He ends up not only collecting on her life insurance money, but he sues the hospital for wrongful death.” And this occurred after Randolph had recorded Frances reciting her will on videotape. Not long after her death, he married Leona Stapleton, but she died of cancer soon after their nuptials. “I went to 10 states and was embedded with [investigators], but what was surprising to me was when I told everyone we met that I was with NBC News, almost every witness in every state said, ‘Oh yeah, you can film this,’” Slepian says, suggesting to him that they all believed Randolph was guilty and wanted to help catch him. “The extraordinary thing is that as we traveled digging into his past, it felt like we were walking into the present.” The pile of evidence and statements was finally enough to take Randolph into custody. He was arrested in January 2009, effectively hiding out at his mother’s home in Utah, where police had to drag him through the snow to their vehicle, an incident Slepian recorded on camera. By then, Slepian had developed a rapport with Randolph over the phone, gleaning insight into his behavior throughout the investigation. But his access only deepened once Randolph was detained and Slepian obtained his permission to keep the camera rolling. Over the next few years, Randolph became convinced he could sway public opinion. He repeatedly delayed his 2008 murder trial, fired lawyers at the last minute, and tried to keep Gault’s case from the ‘80s out of the court. “It was just all kind of a big challenge or game for him,” Slepian says. “He turned suspicion into entertainment and would use his charm and the uncomfortable charisma to camouflage the suspicion.” Looking back, it became clear to Slepian that Randolph had learned from his first murder trial. Given that Tarantino tried to burn Randolph on the witness stand, Slepian came to believe Randolph had killed Miller to eliminate loose ends. Still, he never felt confident that a jury would see it the same way. “The more the facts around him grew darker and more absolute,” Slepian says, “the more he seemed to grow confident in his ability to shape the narrative. Francis Randolph in The Widower Courtesy of Netflix Throughout this time, Slepian had struggled to tap into Randolph’s impenetrable psyche and cocky personality. The Dateline producer worked to remain unbiased, in part to keep Randolph talking throughout his stay in Clark County jail. “We’re going to become infamous together,” Randolph tells Slepian in the documentary, further creeping him out. “The things that he said were so unsettling so often,” Slepian says. “But the unsettling part wasn't what he was saying, it was how comfortable he was saying it.” Slepian had spent much of his previous work fighting for inmates who had been unjustly imprisoned. The irony of taking on this case was stark. “My focus normally is on people who are screaming into the wind from a prison cell who have had an injustice done to them and they can't get anybody to listen. They feel like the system has manipulated them,” he says. “This was kind of the same, except that Randolph was the one manipulating the system. He played the bureaucracy like a piano and kept winning over and over again. And that to me speaks volumes about the system as a whole.” In June 2017, eight years after Causse’s death, Randolph’s case finally went to trial, where he was found guilty of conspiracy to commit murder and two counts of first-degree murder and was sentenced to death. But that wouldn’t be the end. Three years later, the Nevada Supreme Court reversed the conviction and death sentence, ruling that the trial should not have allowed evidence from Gault’s case—just like Randolph had told Slepian. “I put nothing past him,” Slepian says. “It really is because of him that the trial was delayed for nearly a decade. And I think he was waiting for witnesses [to die].” But during his retrial in 2023, Randolph couldn’t pull any more strings. O’Kelley, who, according to Slepian, had “encyclopedic” knowledge about the case, testified and laid out all the inconsistencies in Causse’s case. His testimony, combined with Randolph’s performative reenactment video the night of the murders, convinced the jury of his guilt. “Had it not been for Dean O'Kelly, Thomas Randolph would likely be free today,” Slepian says. “I think Randolph saw the camera as giving him a stage. But it also gave detectives a record.” Randolph, now 71, was sentenced to 60 years to life at the Lovelock Correctional Center in Nevada. And, as noted in the documentary, he’s had multiple appeals denied, including one in early September. Slepian, who still calls Randolph in prison, notes that the Black Widower continues to claim his innocence and has people on the outside handing out flyers around the courthouse about his case. At this point their conversations remain strictly about his legal case, but Slepian is glad that he’s kept his word and remained objective throughout the years. Otherwise, he never would have gotten to share this story. “I had to keep my footing, just listening, knowing why I was there, and using the archive of him over time to create a record,” he says. “The archive itself became a witness to him over time. Now, the whole world gets to know who he is right now.”
TIME
Cornell Sex Assault Case Raises Questions About Title IX Accountability and New York’s Intoxicated Consent Law
2026-10-01

Cornell Sex Assault Case Raises Questions About Title IX Accountability and New York’s Intoxicated Consent Law

TIME | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Merchandise for sale inside the Cornell Store at the Cornell University campus in Ithaca, N.Y., on April 11, 2023. Two years after a woman alleged a gang rape at Cornell University, renewed attention to the case and the limited consequences faced by the alleged perpetrators has raised questions about what higher education owes students who experience sexual assault on campus as well as how consent and intoxication are understood under New York law. A woman identified as Jane Doe filed a civil lawsuit on Sept. 16, alleging that she was sexually assaulted, gang-raped, and pressured to take drugs and drink alcohol by seven members of the Chi Phi fraternity on the night of Oct. 19, 2024. The lawsuit is filed against the fraternity, Doe’s sorority chapter, and Cornell University; it names Matthew Ingalls, Johnathan Newell, Winston Lee, Gillio Lopes, Diego Sarabia, Scott Norris, and Scott Kretzschmar as defendants. Prosecutors said this week they were reopening a criminal investigation into the case after declining to press charges originally. Separate attorneys for Kretzschmar and Lopes denied the allegations in statements to ABC, while Norris, via an attorney, denied engaging in sexual activity or drug use but acknowledged that he sent a message included in the complaint. When sexual assault allegations of this kind occur on campus, they can be addressed in three distinct ways: through school disciplinary proceedings, criminal proceedings, and civil lawsuits. Doe’s civil lawsuit alleges that Cornell failed to protect her. Her attorney has separately criticized authorities’ handling of the criminal investigation, although observers say what’s known as the “voluntary intoxication loophole” in state law could limit what prosecutors are able to charge. Here’s what to know about how the case was handled—by Cornell and the criminal justice system—before, and what could happen next. Doe reported the alleged assault to campus police in November 2024. According to her complaint, Cornell began its Title IX investigation on Jan. 14, 2025. Title IX is a law dating back to 1972 that is meant to, among other things, ensure that a college investigates when there is sexual assault on campus. Central to this premise is that Title IX is meant to ensure that sexual violence does not impede a student’s access to education. Read More: Trump’s Title IX Announcement Is Cruel. But It Does Not Change the Law Doe’s attorney has said that Cornell expelled two of the seven accused students. Cornell has confirmed that its disciplinary process resulted in expulsions and suspensions, but has not disclosed individual outcomes. The lawsuit alleges negligence, breach of contract, and violations of New York law. Cornell responded in a statement last week, saying that it could not confirm the individual disciplinary outcomes due to student privacy laws. However, the university clarified on Monday that “none of the individuals charged were offered the opportunity to write essays as a sole consequence of their involvement.” “We take sexual violence extremely seriously,” the Sept. 21 statement said. “The Presidential Task Force on Campus Sexual Assault was launched shortly after this incident and issued a REPORT in March 2026.” In a statement Monday, Kyle Kimball, Cornell’s vice president for university relations, said Chi Phi’s Cornell chapter closed in 2024 and the fraternity remains barred from campus. But women’s rights advocates believe that the school might not have fulfilled its legal obligations in investigating the incident and taking appropriate actions toward those involved. Title IX is a federal civil rights law meant to prohibit sex-based discrimination in any education program or activity receiving federal financial assistance. Since Title IX was introduced, courts have found that the law requires institutions to ensure that sexual violence does not impede a student’s access to education. “The reason why Title IX addresses sexual harassment, including sexual assault, is because of the very real impact that has on students’ ability to access education,” Shiwali Patel, a Title IX expert with the National Women’s Law Center, tells TIME. According to the Rape, Abuse & Incest National Network (RAINN), students who experience sexual assault are more likely to earn lower GPAs in school and are more likely to drop out of school altogether. Patel points to Doe’s attorney’s account of her efforts to leave Cornell after the alleged assault. In a Sept. 28 interview with CNN, Thomas Giuffra—Jane Doe’s lawyer—said Cornell refused her family’s tuition-refund request, leaving her to spend another semester on campus. “She was trying to leave the institution; she didn’t want to be there,” Patel says. Cornell told People that it works with students seeking to take leave or withdraw and “regularly refunds payments if students are no longer enrolled.” Giuffra also told CNN that Doe is no longer in college as of 2026. Universities also have obligations to offer support regardless of a formal complaint or a finding of culpability. These can include counseling, academic adjustments, and housing or schedule changes. Patel says it remains unclear whether Cornell fully complied with its Title IX obligations because the supportive measures it provided Doe are not publicly known. Regardless, the situation raises questions about what more schools can do for students, Patel says, providing some examples of the types of questions that a university might ask in the aftermath of this kind of incident: “Does she need extensions on exams? Does she need some time off, some leave? Does she need a housing shift if the fraternity house and her sorority house are close together? Does she need counseling? Did they provide a stay away order? What kind of support were they giving her to make sure she wouldn’t have to be retriggered and traumatized by running into them?” Title IX also requires schools to investigate allegations through a reliable, impartial process—independently of criminal court systems, and using a lower burden of proof to determine if a student violated campus policy. Cornell University publicly disclosed that it was investigating the allegation on Nov. 8, 2024, the same day Doe went to the police, according to the lawsuit. The university sent an alert to students regarding a report of sexual assault and coercion into taking drugs at a fraternity house. The same day, Cornell suspended the Chi Phi fraternity. In response to an inquiry from TIME, Cornell said that its “Office of Civil Rights and the Office of Student Conduct and Community Standards investigated the allegations against the individuals and the fraternity consistent with university policies and Title IX.” “The university’s Title IX investigation was conducted over several months and included hearings where a panel of trained faculty and staff heard evidence over multiple days,” the university’s statement continued. “The complainant and respondents had the opportunity to testify and present evidence in the university’s investigation.” Cornell’s handling of the case unfolded amid shifting federal rules on campus sexual misconduct. A court vacated the Biden Administration’s Title IX rule on Jan. 9, 2025—five days before Cornell began its formal investigation, according to Doe’s complaint. The university held hearings that May, making the rules it applied a key question in assessing its response. The Biden Administration had expanded Title IX regulations in April 2024—taking effect in August 2024—broadening the definition of sexual harassment and extending federal protections. On Jan. 9, 2025, however, a federal district court issued a decision vacating the 2024 Biden rule nationwide. This week, the Trump Administration formally restored the 2020 Title IX regulations to the Code of Federal Regulations. Those rules, introduced during President Donald Trump’s first term, had already been enforced since January 2025. “The Trump rule essentially narrowed the definition of sexual harassment to make it more burdensome for a complainant to actually complain of something that meets a definition,” Patel says of the differences between the Biden-era and Trump-era Title IX rules. The 2020 rule requires hostile-environment sexual harassment to be “severe, pervasive, and objectively offensive,” a narrower standard than the Biden-era rule. Sexual assault, however, is covered separately and does not have to meet that threshold—and a direct link between recent revisions and the Cornell case has not been established. “It also sets limits on what schools can respond to under Title IX based on the location of the assault, harassment, and based on the complainant’s status,” she adds. “This is going a huge step backwards because it is allowing schools to do less in the face of sexual assault.” Another difference between the 2020 and 2024 rules was that the latter required schools to proactively monitor educational programs for sex discrimination, identify and address barriers to reporting, and take prompt, effective steps to prevent discrimination from recurring. Patel argues that a university’s ability to prevent and respond to sexual assault is hindered by this “ping-ponging of Title IX protections based on the administration that’s in power.” “We need to have a legislative fix,” she argues. “There needs to be some clarity through legislation to prevent this type of thing from happening.” Nancy Cantalupo, an assistant professor at Wayne State University Law School, says that this type of case highlights issues within the government itself—what she describes as “failures of those in the current administration and certain previous administrations, as well as the courts—led by the U.S. Supreme Court—to interpret and enforce Title IX in a manner that is consistent with Title IX’s purposes.” Namely, she says, that involves preventing gender discrimination in education. Survivor advocates describe Title IX as an alternative form of recourse to the criminal legal system, emphasizing its capacity to address other harms of sexual violence, including its impact on educational access. In written testimony to a Senate roundtable discussion of Title IX in 2014, the Victim Rights Law Center said the law “is a mechanism to enforce a victim’s civil rights and not a tool of the criminal justice system.” Where prosecutors are tasked with focusing on the accused’s accountability and the community’s interests, the Victim Rights Law Center said Title IX is meant to address victims’ equal educational rights. The two processes also apply different standards of proof, at times leading a prosecutor to decline charges because they do not believe the evidence can support a criminal conviction. “The View” co-host Sunny Hostin, who said she prosecuted sex crimes for a decade, defended the initial decision not to prosecute. On the show’s Tuesday episode, the former assistant U.S. attorney said, “under New York law, voluntary intoxication or drug use that merely impairs judgment does not legally constitute an inability to consent to sexual activity.” “There is no way,” she added, “according to [Doe’s] statement in 2024, that any prosecutor would have brought charges.” Under the state’s rape laws, lack of consent can be established through force, an explicit refusal of sex, physical helplessness such as unconsciousness, or mental incapacitation. The definition of mental incapacitation excludes incapacity caused by voluntarily consuming drugs or alcohol, leading to a restriction that lawmakers and advocates call the “voluntary intoxication loophole.” “The current law sees it in black and white: Either you’re stone cold sober or you’re totally comatose,” says Ann Olivarius, an attorney who represents victims of sexual misconduct and discrimination in employment. “Life is not like that, and certainly not in college. We all know how easily our faculties are undermined by drugs and alcohol.” Even if, under the law, you’re too impaired to operate heavy machinery or be responsible for children, Olivarius says, “if you’re slurring, stumbling, or visibly disoriented, you are still considered capable of giving consent for sex.” The law’s limitations have been a focal point of the debate over whether the prosecutor should, or could, have brought charges in 2024. Tompkins County District Attorney Matthew Van Houten reopened the investigation and said the case would be presented to a grand jury for their consideration. But he defended his initial decision not to charge the alleged perpetrators in a statement on Monday, citing New York state laws on intoxication and consent. According to Van Houten, Doe’s sworn statement in November 2024 “did not allege that she was drugged against her will or gang raped. On the contrary, Jane Doe’s statement described her participation in drug use and sexual conduct as voluntary, conscious, and consensual.” The civil lawsuit, however, states that she was incapacitated when frat members sexually assaulted her and thus incapable of consenting. It also claims that she was “pressured” and “plied” with drugs, and that certain acts were done “without her consent” as well as some “forced” on her. Olivarius, who was also a plaintiff in the landmark Alexander v. Yale case nearly half a century ago that established sexual harassment is a form of sex discrimination under Title IX, tells TIME that the nature of the law can also lead to victim-blaming questions: “They say to her, ‘Were you drunk? Why did you drink?’ … It’s all on her. The New York Times reported in May that people who voluntarily drank or took drugs faced a high burden when pursuing rape charges. Legal advocates have for years argued that the law’s limitations underscore the need for reform, especially as advocacy efforts have increasingly spotlighted how intoxication can impair a person’s ability to consent to sex. The New York State Assembly is forming a working group to review the state’s rape laws in light of the civil lawsuit, State Assembly Speaker Carl Heastie, a Democrat, announced on Wednesday. The group, composed of 14 Democratic Assembly members, will be expected to report its findings and legislative recommendations to Heastie and the Assembly Democratic majority conference by the end of the year. A bill addressing the voluntary intoxication exclusion passed the state Senate unanimously in June, but remains stalled in the Assembly. Earlier versions passed the Senate in 2020, 2021, 2023, 2024, and 2025, but none passed the Assembly. The proposals to change the law date back to legislation introduced by then-Sen. Alessandra Biaggi, a Democrat, in 2019. New York is one of 19 states whose rape or sexual assault laws recognize intoxication as mental incapacitation only when the person became intoxicated without their knowledge or consent, according to research by the Associated Press and a report published in the Journal of the American Academy of Psychiatry and the Law. Several states have changed their laws in recent years to also protect victims incapacitated by voluntary intoxication. In 2021, lawmakers in Minnesota expanded the definition of mental incapacitation to include voluntary intoxication, after the state Supreme Court overturned a sexual assault conviction under the narrower definition. Michigan also revised its laws in 2023 to broaden its definition of mental incapacitation. Last year, Texas expanded its law to cover sexual acts committed when the perpetrator knows the other person is too impaired to consent, regardless of how that person became intoxicated. California also tightened its sentencing laws in 2016 following the sexual assault case involving Stanford student Brock Turner. The state barred probation and suspended sentences for certain sexual offenses involving victims who were incapable of consenting because of intoxication. There are three possible ways that this case may move forward. Van Houten said his office plans to present the criminal case to a grand jury, which would determine whether an indictment is warranted. “Seeking justice sometimes requires us to reconsider or reopen cases when we are provided with additional evidence,” Van Houten said in his Monday statement. He told ABC that his office wanted to determine whether additional evidence, not considered at the time, would change its assessment, potentially including a screenshot included in the civil complaint of an alleged Snapchat message between the fraternity members that referred to Doe as “free p-ssy.” After the woman’s initial police report, she reportedly told police a week later that she was “completely and totally incapacitated,” and could “say with 100% confidence I was raped.” A transcript obtained by CBS of a police interview on Nov. 14 and 15, 2024, showed that the woman described being “physically hit very, very hard,” and describing the incident as “coercion.” Van Houten told CBS that he had not seen the transcript of the interview, and that his office had relied on the sworn statement when it declined to bring charges. Separately, Doe’s civil lawsuit seeks monetary damages from the seven men and other defendants, including Cornell University. A court date has not been set. New York Gov. Kathy Hochul on Tuesday also called for an independent investigation into how Cornell University handled the allegations when they arose in 2024. “The allegations in this case are nothing short of horrifying. For any student to come forward after an experience like this takes extraordinary courage. No student should ever be left wondering whether the institutions meant to protect them will listen and act,” the governor’s statement said. “That is why I am calling for outside counsel to conduct a transparent, independent review of the university’s response to determine whether Cornell met every obligation to protect its students and pursue justice.” The individuals who might be tasked with such an investigation have yet to be named; however, the outcomes could help shape how similar investigations into sexual assault cases are handled on campuses both in and beyond New York. “This case,” Olivarius tells TIME, “ may change the conversation.”

Mike Flanagan's Timely Carrie Is the First Worthwhile Adaptation in 50 Years
2026-10-01

Mike Flanagan's Timely Carrie Is the First Worthwhile Adaptation in 50 Years

TIME | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Summer H. Howell as Carrie in the new Prime Video series Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Everyone knows what happens in Carrie. Fifty-two years after its publication, the novel that ignited Stephen King’s career has so thoroughly engulfed the culture, you’d have to be almost as sheltered as Carietta White herself not to have absorbed its most indelible images. The locker-room tampon gauntlet and “Plug it up!” chant. Carrie’s smothering holy-roller mom, locking her up in prayer-closet solitary confinement. The eerily unlikely prom date. The swinging bucket that has haunted every coronation of school-dance royalty in film and TV since Brian De Palma first adapted the book in 1976. It’s a tale as old as time: Warped by her mother’s zealotry and scapegoated since childhood by her classmates, 16-year-old Carrie has a late, traumatic menarche that reactivates her latent telekinetic abilities. Then, after accepting a pity date to the prom, she’s crowned queen and immediately gets pig’s blood dumped on her head by a resentful bully. The humiliation causes her to lay waste to the school and then the town, using her powers to exact revenge. King’s book and De Palma’s film became classics of the horror genre. No less venerable a critic than Pauline Kael wrote of Sissy Spacek’s Oscar-nominated portrayal: “I don’t see how this performance could be any better.” Yet in the 21st century, new interpreters keep trying to improve upon it. With platforms scrambling to capitalize on popular IP, it’s no mystery why this would sound like a good idea to Hollywood executives. But something else must be inspiring high-minded filmmakers like Boys Don’t Cry director Kimberly Peirce, who released a divisive remake in 2013, to keep resurrecting Carrie. The fourth screen adaptation of the novel (unless you count the pitiful 1999 sequel, The Rage: Carrie 2) is an eight-episode Prime Video series created by Mike Flanagan, who is known both for riffing on horror touchstones in Netflix hits like The Haunting of Hill House and The Fall of the House of Usher and for his features based on King novels like Gerald’s Game and Doctor Sleep. Premiering Oct. 7, his ambitious Carrie is the first that manages to fully get out from under the shadow of De Palma’s. Yet it confirms a gradual shift in how the story is being told. What was once an account of an abusive mother and an evil bully unwittingly uniting to create a lethal monster has become, with each new incarnation, more like an indictment of society as a whole. Summer H. Howell, left, and Samantha Sloyan in Carrie (2026) Liane Hentscher—Prime All three Carrie movies are mostly faithful to the novel, dutifully reproducing every major plot point and set piece, as well as a lot of dialogue. King’s writing is remarkably cinematic, which is probably why he’s our most adapted living author. But every Carrie is also a product of its time. De Palma, whose masterfully crafted version turns 50 in November, seems to lust after and fear the female body in equal measure—a common response within what was then a male-dominated genre to the feminist upheavals of the ’70s. It’s hard to imagine a contemporary adaptation pairing its opening credits with a steamy, slow-motion parade of nude high school girls in their locker room. A 2002 made-for-TV movie written by cult creator Bryan Fuller (Hannibal) is a relic of an era when the only things teen girls loved more than low-rise denim were movies where female misfits get glow ups (She’s All That, 10 Things I Hate About You, Jawbreaker). Just over a decade later, Peirce incorporated smartphones and YouTube, as well as an emerging pop-feminist sensibility that traced the derangement of Carrie’s mom, Margaret (played here by Julianne Moore), to childbirth trauma and found a palpably empowered Carrie actively choosing who to kill and who to save. You can find support for each of these readings in King’s own reflections. “The book is, in its more adult implications, an uneasy masculine shrinking from a future of female equality,” he wrote in his 1981 nonfiction book Danse Macabre. “For me, Carrie White is a sadly misused teenager, an example of the sort of person whose spirit is so often broken for good in that pit of man- and woman-eaters that is your normal suburban high school. But she's also Woman, feeling her powers for the first time and, like Samson, pulling down the temple on everyone in sight.” In her influential 1992 study Men, Women, and Chain Saws: Gender in the Modern Horror Film, the scholar Carol J. Clover suggests that this tangle of intentions explains the broad and, in some ways, contradictory appeal of Carrie across genders. Its protagonist is, at once, a projection of female power and male fear and the universal horror of bullying. Despite feeling mostly redundant, the two features released after Clover’s book shared a new emphasis. De Palma’s stylish ’70s chiller played up the ghoulishness of Margaret (a bellowing, vicious Piper Laurie) and of Carrie’s chief tormentor at school, the sadistic mean girl Chris Hargensen (Nancy Allen), as well as the otherworldly oddness of Spacek’s lead. But Fuller’s and especially Peirce’s films sand down those characters’ excesses, revealing motivations and vulnerabilities that make them harder to hate or fear. (The latter faced criticism for casting the conventionally beautiful, preternaturally confident Chloë Grace Moretz in the title role.) At the same time, we see the condescending assumptions behind the actions of ostensibly heroic figures like Sue Snell, the popular girl who tries to atone for hazing Carrie by sending her boyfriend to the prom with her. In these movies, Carrie’s reign of terror is the ultimate expression of the school’s brokenness. As the critic Matt Zoller Seitz wrote in a favorable review of the 2013 version: “The first Carrie was horror. This is tragedy.” Bullies face off against their prey in Carrie (2026) Prime If the 21st century Carrie movies spoke to a post-Columbine era of school shootings, then Flanagan’s series is a product of the Trump decade, in which seemingly every aspect of American life has fractured along bipartisan political lines. The educational realm has become a crucible for debates around censorship, technology, parental rights, gun control, LGBTQ inclusion, critical race theory, the teaching of science vs. religion, social media. Which explains how Flanagan and his creative team, which includes many female writers and directors, found in this slight story eight episodes’ worth of material. Without omitting any of the moments King fans will anticipate, they depart pretty boldly from the text. This Carrie opens with the death of Carrie’s conspiracy-theorist father (who, in previous versions, she never got to know) in a bizarre accident on his construction site. Somehow, no one knows he had a wife (Flanagan regular Samantha Sloyan, locating the wounded soul of a character who’s always been more of a caricature) and daughter (a movingly fragile Summer H. Howell) until they’re found cowering at home, in the closet where they pray. The previously homeschooled Carrie is forced to enroll at the local public high school while Margaret gets a job at a soulless big-box store, where her refusal to ring up a sex toy gets her demoted to cleaning toilets. An almost saintly figure who believes in kindness most of all, Carrie develops complicated friendships with smart, benevolent Sue (Siena Agudong) and Chris (Alison Thornton)—who has been spiraling since her mom moved in with a classmate’s dad and her own father’s financial woes have jeopardized her college plans. What she doesn’t know is that Chris is behind an anonymous social media account that posted footage of her locker-room nightmare before expanding to target practically everyone at school, teachers included. A constant nuisance to the administration, Margaret harangues Principal Grayle (Matthew Lillard) to intervene. But he dithers, questioning whether the free speech of kids on a platform external to school is his jurisdiction. Matthew Lillard in Carrie (2026) Robert Falconer—Prime The problem isn’t that Grayle doesn’t care; he’s a kindhearted guy who wants the best for his students. So do other adults, like the girls’ young gym teacher (Amber Midthunder), who’s horrified by the “Plug it up!” incident, and empathetic school counselor (Somebody Somewhere sweetheart Tim Bagley). Even Margaret, an archetypal Karen with MAHA tendencies who becomes a vlogging parents’ rights crusader, is sincerely trying to help a daughter she cherishes. She’s not wrong to point out that a world where, for example, children are shot in their classrooms is an abomination. But meaning well is not enough. Fights, protests, and parental panics ensue. By prom night, whose carnage is now so unsurprising that Flanagan flashes forward to it in the opening scene, the place has already metaphorically exploded. Carrie only makes it literal. For everything of value it adds to the story, Flanagan’s take can still feel a bit cluttered. There are too many interconnected framing devices, from on-camera interviews with survivors of Carrie’s massacre to history lessons on Joan of Arc. If you watched House of Usher, which filters the downfall of a Sackler-like family of opioid profiteers through the sieve of Poe’s Gothic tale, you’re probably aware that Flanagan’s social commentary can be blunt. His Carrie can get bogged down in meetings and assemblies where characters representing different facets of Trump-era vitriol and complacency monologue on what’s really wrong with the world today. Yet at the core of this series is a perceptive diagnosis of that problem. More than individual bad actors who need to be brought down, there are people like Grayle and Margaret and Sue, whose pure intentions are undermined by complacency, indecision, disinformation, conformity, fear—all of it amplified to a potentially global scale by social media. Even Chris is lashing out as a direct result of her family’s dissolution and her father’s money troubles, which erode the class privileges to which she feels entitled. Amid extreme rhetoric, Flanagan implies, everyone who doesn’t work hard to avert atrocities is complicit in them. And there's precious little evidence that most of us are doing much to examine that complicity. Tragedy becomes, specifically, a tragedy of the commons as selfishness metastasizes into a scourge on the entire town. And Carrie becomes something between Frankenstein’s monster and Jesus Christ: an innocent persecuted for the sins of all mankind, but one whose all-too-human impulse to seek retribution incinerates any hope of forgiveness.

What Trump Has Built
2026-10-01

What Trump Has Built

TIME | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump on the newly built helipad at the White House in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Donald Trump struts onto the new White House helipad, an 80,000-sq.-ft. expanse of carved granite on the South Lawn. “Can you believe this?” he says, taking in the scene, the Washington Monument rising in the distance. The President lingers over the details of the project with obvious pleasure, pointing out the gray stone imported from Italy and describing how five separate sections were fitted together, with measurements he says are precise to an eighth of an inch. Behind him rises a bigger renovation project: the new White House ballroom, its concrete skeleton consuming the former East Wing. Lawsuits have followed the endeavor, alleging that Trump demolished the historic structure and began building its replacement without congressional authorization or the required federal reviews. He kept building anyway. Trump points to the walls, calling them “nuclear concrete” (the sort, he says, used in nuclear facilities), and describes a military complex beneath the ballroom that will plunge six stories underground. There will be five separate floors, he says, with one side made entirely of glass, 6 or 8 in. thick. He holds his hands apart to demonstrate. “The steel goes up next week.” As we walk toward the Oval Office, Trump stops several times to inspect his handiwork. There are freshly painted doors, new gold doorknobs, the Rose Garden lawn ripped out and replaced with a Mar-a-Lago-style patio, where he now presides over dinners at what he calls the Rose Garden Club. “People say, why do you spend so much time on this?” he says. “But I find it relaxing.” Later, he returns to the subject again. “It’s what I do best,” he muses aloud. “I say, am I a better builder or a politician?” One can understand Trump’s fixation on his construction projects. At one of the lowest points of his presidency, he is absorbed with the physical details of a world he can remake to his liking. There is something revealing in the contrast. Here, he can still point to a wall and say how thick it should be, decide where the grass ends and the stone begins, make demands and have them fulfilled. With his presidency seemingly spinning off course, Trump has busied himself with things he can control. Outside these gates, events have become increasingly resistant to Trump’s will. The war in Iran, launched without congressional authorization and initially described by Trump as a four-to-six-week undertaking, is seven months old when we meet, with no end in sight. It has sent oil and gasoline prices soaring and convulsed the global economy. Inflationary pressures compounded by Trump’s tariffs have pushed the Federal Reserve—now led by his handpicked chairman—to raise interest rates over his objections, with another hike possible before year’s end. Mortgage rates have climbed to 7.5%, worsening an already punishing housing market. The political coalition that returned Trump to power has frayed, with signs of discontent among independents, rural voters, and parts of the Latino electorate, even as his GOP base remains substantially loyal. A Reuters/Ipsos poll in September put his approval at 32%, the lowest of his political career; a recent Wall Street Journal survey recorded the lowest ratings of any President heading into midterm elections since 1990. Republican candidates have distanced themselves from his military actions and his deportation campaign. Voters have recoiled at his laissez-faire approach to the artificial intelligence industry and his accumulation of wealth while in office through crypto deals, thousands of disclosed stock trades, and an expanding empire of Trump-branded developments stretching from Saudi Arabia and Qatar to India and Vietnam. Inside the Administration, senior advisers increasingly recognize that he has passed the apogee of his second-term power. Across the Republican Party, officials are preparing for the likelihood of losing the House—and perhaps the Senate­—in the midterms. After two years spent almost entirely on offense, Trump may soon be forced onto defense. Democratic control of Congress would bring investigations and subpoenas, hearings, and potentially a third round of impeachment proceedings. Cabinet officials could be hauled before Congress. Trump describes the prospect in characteristic terms. “They’ll probably go after me,” he says, “and I’ll go after them.” Yet with so much at stake—for the country and the world, for his party and his own power—Trump can seem curiously preoccupied. During a 75-minute Oval Office interview with TIME, he was discursive and grievance-fueled, veering from the crises of his presidency to resentments old and emerging. He said he regretted appointing Neil Gorsuch, Brett Kavanaugh, and Amy Coney Barrett to the Supreme Court, after they ruled against him on tariffs, mail ballots, and birthright citizenship. “I gave them the position of a lifetime,” he said, “and they voted against me often.” On his expectation of fealty, he was unapologetic: “I do like loyalty, yeah.” Again and again, he returned to the condition of the White House itself, complaining that his predecessors had left the building in disrepair and deriding Barack Obama as someone who “doesn’t know if something’s dirty or clean.” Statues are pictured in the Rose Garden of the White House, Friday, Sept. 11, 2026, in Washington, D.C. Mark Schiefelbein—AP Photo Trump dismissed widespread opposition to the rapid build-out of data centers, including among his own base, as a prescription for a poorer America. When pressed on the limits of his power, Trump left himself ample room: He would not rule out declaring a national emergency or invoking the Insurrection Act around the midterms, nor promise to refrain from bringing wrecking balls to the Kennedy Center without congressional approval. “I’m not committing to anything,” he says. “We’re going to see how it plays out in court.” In Trump’s telling, he is simply doing what has to be done, even when that means breaking foundational promises to his voters. He campaigned as a president of peace, but is now waging a war he says was necessary to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon. He points to Venezuela, where his removal of Nicolás Maduro and the elevation of Delcy Rodríguez have brought the country into Washington’s orbit, as proof that his projection of U.S. power can produce results. He touts most-­favored-nation drug pricing and restricting institutional investors from buying single-­family homes as measures that will lower costs for Americans. New GDP and inflation figures suggest the economy remains strong.When asked why he remains consumed with construction projects so removed from the country’s concerns, Trump offers a rationale: “The White House is a very special symbol. It’s a symbol of America. It’s a symbol of our greatness.” He speaks of his building projects in the language of legacy—monuments meant to endure long after he is gone. But while Trump obsesses over stonework and fixtures, gold and glass, the more important parts of his legacy are being built too—in the decisions he makes from inside these walls, and the consequences unfolding far beyond them. In late April, the President summoned Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and a retinue of military and national security officials to the Oval Office. By Trump’s own timeline, the war with Iran was supposed to be over already, with a new regime in place and a nuclear program throttled. But the Islamic Republic had proved resilient and resourceful, choking off oil through the Strait of Hormuz and sending tremors through the global economy. Now there was another problem. Trump had learned from a New York Times report that the U.S. military was running low on certain munitions after firing more than 1,200 Patriot missile interceptors. To replenish the losses, the Pentagon was drawing on assets stationed in Asia and Europe, potentially weakening American defenses in other theaters. “What the f-ck is this?” Trump said to Hegseth, according to two officials familiar with the exchange, handing him a printout of the story. According to those officials, Hegseth borrowed one of Trump’s favorite terms of derision, telling the President it was “fake news.” The Secretary of Defense insisted that the reporters had taken the facts out of context and exaggerated them to make the Administration look bad. But the reporting emerged from an official DOD inspector general’s assessment. (A spokesperson for Hegseth denied the exchange.)Trump’s frustration went beyond munition stores. The war was polling miserably. At the time, 13 U.S. service members had been killed and nearly 400 wounded. (Those numbers would increase to 19 killed and more than 800 wounded by the end of September.) Gasoline prices had risen more than 40% since the opening attacks on Feb. 28. The conflict had already cost more than $25 billion, and the consequences were beginning to bleed into the midterm campaign. The President found himself without an exit ramp. That dynamic appears to hold true: Instead of concluding the war, Trump tells TIME, he may ramp up the bombing after the midterms if they can’t reach a satisfactory deal. “Because by annihilating Iran, we’ve created peace in the world,” he says. “With Iran, I don’t think you could ever have peace.” In recent months, Trump’s relationship with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has grown more acrimonious, according to a senior Administration official. Netanyahu urged Trump toward war with Tehran but has since complicated Trump’s efforts to end it, including by striking Hezbollah targets in Lebanon after Trump told him not to, multiple U.S. officials tell TIME. Trump says he does not believe reports that Netanyahu was warned by Egypt and the UAE ahead of the Oct. 7 attacks, but stops short of endorsing him for re-election as Israelis prepare to vote in late October for the first time since the worst security failure in the country’s history. Asked whether he might work better with Netanyahu’s leading challenger, former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, Trump leaves the possibility open. “Well, I don’t know,” he says. “I don’t hear bad things about him.”The decision to attack Iran was a hinge point in Trump’s second term. His first year back in power had unfolded as a frenetic exercise in presidential will, a barrage of executive actions and dismantled guardrails that had largely gone according to plan. Then his second year opened with the raid on Caracas and capture of Maduro, a daring operation that reinforced Trump’s conviction that audacity could succeed where caution had failed. The subsequent attack on Tehran would test the limits of that conviction—and reveal how quickly confidence born of success could lead to overreach. A cascade of crises followed. Though Trump has dismissed the pain of rising gas prices, he concedes the war has depleted a subset of U.S. munitions. “Some forms of ammunition are a little bit lower than other forms,” he says. Trump says he has struck deals with leading defense manufacturers, including Raytheon and Lockheed Martin, to replenish the arsenal and expand production. “They’re building four, five, six plants,” he says, citing Patriot and THAAD anti-ballistic-missile facilities. “They’re building tremendous amounts of capability.”When asked what this will cost American taxpayers, Trump offers a curious metric: less, he says, than America makes in a single month from Venezuelan oil. Yet Americans have so far felt little benefit from that arrangement. Major U.S. oil companies have returned to Venezuela and begun extracting from the world’s largest proven crude reserves, but gas prices remain at record highs for September. Meanwhile, Trump’s tariff regime strained America’s global relationships and reverberated through the economy. Trump points to low unemployment, robust consumer spending, and a stock market at record highs as evidence that his economic program is working. By those measures, the economy has remained strong. But they coexist with a harsher reality: the cost of everyday life remains painfully high.By summer, Trump was growing angrier with U.S. allies that had refused to come to America’s aid against Iran—from the European powers and South Korea to Canada. Trump has directed much of his ire over the issue at America’s neighbor to the north, according to a close aide. “He doesn’t like the fact that we essentially protect them because of their proximity to our military might, and doesn’t think they give us enough in return,” says a Trump senior adviser. On July 20, Trump invoked a 1930 statute to impose a 50% tariff on Canadian goods. The trade war with America’s second largest trading partner hit steel, aluminum, and lumber especially hard, disrupting supply chains and raising the cost of materials that went into everything from factories to new homes. President Trump at the White House in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 28, 2026. Dan Winters for TIME Borrowing costs were becoming their own problem. For Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, preventing a sell-off in the bond market from becoming a broader crisis was an increasingly urgent task. By late September, only 25% of Americans believed Trump’s policies had improved the economy, according to a Wall Street Journal poll, while 60% said they had worsened it. Trump had returned to office promising cheaper energy, lower prices, and an economic boom. Instead, voters were finding the opposite. “It’s not lost on any of us,” a Trump adviser tells TIME. “When voters aren’t happy with the economy, they tend to vote against the party in charge.” Since returning to office, Trump has marveled at AI’s capabilities. He is increasingly in thrall to the tech executives courting him. In December 2025, Elon Musk returned to the Oval Office for a clandestine meeting. Trump spent hours asking Grok, Musk’s AI chatbot, questions about his presidency and legacy, according to an official present. At the time, Trump was ordering missile strikes against Venezuelan boats he alleged were smuggling drugs into the U.S. He asked Grok how Venezuelans would react if the U.S. captured Maduro. According to the official present, the chatbot responded that Maduro was a repressive and deeply unpopular dictator and that many Venezuelans would likely celebrate his downfall. After Trump ordered the mission to seize Maduro, the following month, celebrations broke out in the streets. Trump, according to officials, came away thinking Grok was ingenious. Along with his fascination with the technology came two related concerns. One was economic: that enthusiasm for AI’s potential was propping up the stock market. The other was geopolitical, reinforced in conversations with industry titans such as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang: that China could gain a decisive advantage over the U.S. if it developed more powerful AI first. Trump was committed to giving American companies the freedom to build faster than their foreign competitors. That meant embracing the unprecedented infrastructure build-out required to support the industry’s computing needs. After watching a cable news segment about the backlash, Trump fired off a Truth Social post. “The only reason that communities throughout the U.S.A. should not want Data Centers is if they want to end up being backwards and poor,” he wrote Aug. 31. “If they want to be successful and rich, with far lower taxes and jobs all over the place, let Data Reign.” Days later, aides showed Trump surveys from his longtime pollster, Tony Fabrizio, showing pervasive anxiety about AI: fears of lost jobs, rising costs, and technology eventually escaping human control. Fellow Republicans facing voters in November were increasingly eager to put distance between themselves and the issue. Trump “­really just didn’t seem to care,” one aide says. “He wants to do what he wants to do. He’s used to generating outrage and then watching everyone eventually crawl back to him. It’s hard to convince him to do anything at this point.” Trump was unfazed by the burgeoning bipartisan calls for new AI regulations. “I don’t have to destroy the industry,” he says. “We’re making a lot of money with that industry. That industry is the Industrial Revolution, but bigger. It’s the internet, but much bigger.” The details don’t appear to concern him. When TIME asks about OpenAI agents’ meddling with federal government websites, Trump responds: “Who breached it? They’re not allowed to do that.” President Trump speaks to the press after a meeting with technology executives about artificial intelligence at the White House in Washington, D.C., on Sept. 29, 2026. Kent Nishimura—AFP/Getty Images Trump’s contribution to the debate about AI safeguards has been to announce the formation of an “AI Force,” which he now wants to rename the “SI Force”—part of his effort to rebrand artificial intelligence as “super intelligence.” Asked what the group will do, Trump responds: “It’s going to be super intelligent.” Pressed further, he explains that it will consist of industry leaders advising him on government policy. On Sept. 29, he met with AI leaders at the White House, where the President and top executives from six leading tech companies signed a “constitution” to police themselves—a covenant of self-regulation in lieu of government oversight. An Administration official present said they left with a “no surprises if something happens” understanding between the White House and the industry. It was an example of how Trump has surrounded himself with industry leaders, seeking their advice, favor, and political donations. A recent CNN poll found 75% of Americans viewed artificial intelligence with more fear and concern than excitement, with broad bipartisan support for greater federal oversight. But Trump waves away AI fears, placing them in a familiar catalogue of what he considers manufactured panics. “They said that our country was going to burn down because of global warming,” he says. “Then they changed the name to climate change because global warming turned out to be a hoax. Russia, Russia, Russia was a hoax.” Asked who, exactly, is perpetrating the supposed hoax of AI concern, Trump throws his hands in the air. “The whole country is based on fake stuff,” he says, “because these people have Trump derangement syndrome.” Inside the Oval Office, Trump’s redesign is impossible to miss. There is gold everywhere, from the decorative plasterwork to the inscriptions on the ceiling and the gilded frames crowding the walls. The room is so dazzling you practically need sunglasses. Trumpian tchotchkes are scattered about: a FIFA “World Peace Prize” sits next to the Resolute Desk, where the President keeps a commendation given to his uncle during his years as an MIT professor. At times, Trump’s train of thought was difficult to keep up with, his conversation moving in streams and eddies before circling back to familiar obsessions. He recounted conversations that sometimes sounded improbable. At one point, Trump claimed the Secret Service had wanted to tear down the Oval Office and rebuild it, only for him to intervene. “I said, ‘My ass, you can’t build a new one. This is a special place.’” Whatever happens in November, people close to Trump expect him to devote more of his remaining presidency to foreign affairs, as second-term Presidents often do, with particular attention to the western hemisphere, where ideologically sympathetic governments have given him opportunities to expand American influence. At home, they expect another emphasis altogether: Trump’s campaign against what he calls “lawfare” and his effort to reshape cultural institutions, including the nation’s museums, in his battle against what he calls “woke” ideology. Looking ahead to the midterms, Trump dismisses unfavorable polls as fake and insists Republicans can prevail. “I think we’ll do OK if I campaign, and I intend to,” he says. So far, he has done little beyond a two-day midterm convention and a few fundraisers, but top advisers say the President is preparing a campaign sprint to help Republicans stave off disaster in the midterms: visiting 32 competitive districts in the next month, with the MAGA-style rallies that powered his political rise. Inside the White House, advisers are already contemplating what a divided Washington would mean. “I think if the worst happens in the midterms, we’ll be forced to think more bipartisan,” one top official says. “I would hope that if we win, we can still do that, because for two years we’ve run a completely partisan play. When you’re looking back on your time, I don’t think that’s what you should be proud of, even if it was successful.”Trump’s political operation boasts a $400 million war chest, but he has only recently begun opening his checkbook. Aides say he has been reluctant to spend because he wants to preserve cash for future elections as a means of keeping his grip on his party. “He wants to have as much money in his account as possible when he’s no longer able to raise the kind of money he can now,” a Trump aide told TIME this fall. “He doesn’t seem to care that much about the midterms, thinks they’re a lost cause anyway, and wants to preserve future possibilities.” Aides are bracing for turnover after the midterms as Trump sheds officials who have frustrated him. A source close to the President says he has openly mused about firing the FBI Director Kash Patel and has expressed frustration with Hegseth. Trump says he intends to protect those who remain. When TIME asks whether he plans to issue pre-emptive pardons to aides and associates before Democrats—or a future Administration—can pursue them, he suggests he will, offering a familiar justification: “Sleepy Joe gave pardons to everybody.”As the conversation moves between war and peace, his own grip on power, and the possibility of impeachment, Trump keeps returning to the trappings of the office. He asks Natalie Harp, his ever present aide, to bring in photographs of earlier White House construction projects, including renovations from the 1930s and the Truman-era reconstruction. He studies the pictures as evidence for his case, lamenting the judges and injunctions now complicating his own efforts. Holding up a photograph of work being performed on the State Dining Room in 1950, Trump complains about a judge he says accused him of destroying the White House. “It’s the sh-t I have to go through,” he says. Toward the end of our interview, I ask what he will miss most when his presidency is over. This time there is no digression, no grievance, no story. Trump does not need a moment to think: “This beautiful room.” —With reporting by Leslie Dickstein, Simmone Shah, and Olivia B. Waxman

Donald Trump Has Appeared on the Cover of TIME More Than Anyone Else

Donald Trump Has Appeared on the Cover of TIME More Than Anyone Else

TIME | 2026-10-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump being photographed by Dan Winters for TIME at the White House in Washington, D.C. on Sept. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It was 35 days before the midterm elections and President Donald Trump wanted to talk about dirt. Standing on the White House lawn, outside the Residence, he told us about the poor conditions his predecessors had left in the world’s most famous home office. Dirty walls. The dirt that visitors, especially those in high heels, confronted when they visited the Rose Garden, before his installation of a patio. Most pressing of all, the dirt that Marine One had to land in before he completed his newest addition to the complex, a 100-ft.-wide helipad. “The happiest people were the pilots,” he said of the change. “They would land in the mud.” The night before, Trump had been greeted at the helipad by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and his wife Cami Clark, who joined the President for a late-night dinner. It was part of another whirlwind week that included a speech before the U.N. General Assembly and hosting Chinese President Xi Jinping and his delegation at the White House—name cards for leading attendees were still on the walk outside the Oval Office when we visited—as well as an Air Force One flyover of the Texas-Tennessee football game, a visit to the Presidents Cup golf tournament outside Chicago, the announcement of a new steel mill in Iowa, and a controversy over the release of taxpayer-funded political ads. As the midterms near, the President is touting successes: low unemployment, economic growth, oil exports rebounding in the Middle East, business opportunities created in Venezuela. But for the moment, Trump seemed stuck in the mud. The costs of housing, health care, and gasoline are increasing at significant rates. His polls are at career lows (he dismissed these numbers as “fake”), Democrats are salivating over the possibility of taking over the House and maybe the Senate, and Trump’s approach to ending an unpopular war with Iran is unfolding as trial and error. The day we met with the President marked seven months since the beginning of the Iran conflict, an engagement he repeatedly told Americans would last only a matter of weeks. Our interview also followed a week in which he had attempted to bar some of our colleagues from reporting on him from the White House and as part of the President’s traveling press corps, a move we have joined our colleagues in objecting to. With Eric Cortellessa's cover story, as part of our commitment to transparency, we are publishing a full transcript of our interview with the President along with a fact-check of his remarks on Time.com. This cover marks a milestone for this magazine and for the President: with 56 appearances, Trump has now been on the cover of TIME more often than any other individual, surpassing President Richard Nixon, who held the title for more than half a century, repeatedly breaking his own record as the Watergate scandal progressed. Trump’s first TIME cover came in 1989, when he appeared to personify that moment’s collective aspiration for luxury. His voice in that story remains familiar to us now: “I have an absolute strategy, but it’s an innate strategy and not definable,” he said. “When you start studying yourself too deeply, you start seeing things that maybe you don’t want to see.” (This cover has hung above a urinal in a men’s room at Mar-a-Lago.) It should come as little surprise that American Presidents (and Jesus) rank among the most regular occupants of TIME’s red border. Eight of the 10 most frequent TIME cover subjects over the past century have been Commanders in Chief. More than 20 different artists and photographers, including Dan Winters, who photographed the President at his helipad for this issue, have contributed Trump covers over the years. The 56 TIME covers featuring Donald Trump. We focus on leadership and influence at TIME, and so Trump has been a constant subject since 2015. Our covers capture his improbable 2016 campaign and first election, his impeachments and legal battles, the disgrace of Jan. 6, his return to power, his foreign policy wins, and all that has come in between. This moment—with Trump tested by a war many counseled against, facing voters who will soon take their first measure of his second presidency—marks another milestone for a man who may be the most consequential leader of this century so far. Trump has not been shy about his interest in being on the cover of this magazine. During a 2025 visit to Israel he was presented with a framed compilation of his previous TIME covers. “Wow, I love it,” he said. “I want to make sure it comes back with us.” On the first day of his first term in office, he stood in front of national-security officials and reflected on the frequency of his cover appearances. “I have been on their cover like 14 or 15 times,” he said. “I think we have the all-time record in the history of TIME magazine. If Tom Brady is on the cover, it’s one time because you won the Super Bowl or something, right? I’ve been on the cover 15 times this year. I don’t think that’s a record that could ever be broken.” (By Jan. 20, 2017, he had appeared 11 times, including eight in 2016.) Fake TIME covers featuring Trump were once a feature of his private clubs. When he celebrated an effort to stop New York City’s congestion pricing in 2025, he circulated another counterfeit cover. And another TIME cover was transfigured and transformed into a favorite meme. While the President covets being on the cover of TIME, he makes it clear he is not always happy with the result, and he has been keeping score: he told us this week that of the 55 previous covers, he has liked 17 of them. Trump is unbounded by history, norm, or custom. For more than a decade now, he has maintained the ability to surprise. To his critics, this leads to shock. To his supporters, delight. He is intent on leaving his imprint on the presidency. That preoccupation with his legacy has been a factor in the burst of renovation and construction projects at the White House and across Washington. He says this work is a form of relaxation. If he cannot control the events of the world, he can control what happens in his own backyard. There is one thing he’d like to leave untouched. Someone from the Secret Service told him they should knock down the Oval Office, he said, and build a new one. “My ass, you can’t build a new one,” he said he responded. “This is a special place.” He also told us he wasn’t sure whether he was a better builder or a better politician. “This whole place was a disgusting mess,” he said of the White House as we left, “and now it’s perfect.”
